Remark 6.6.3 (The log factor is unavoidable). The logarithmic factor in Lemma
6.6.2 is necessary and optimal in general (Exercise 6.37), making Gaussian sym-
metrization weaker than Rademacher’s.
   Get more practice with contraction: prove it for general distributions (Exer-
cise 6.36) and convex functions of norms (Exercise 6.38).


                                               6.7 Notes
The decoupling inequality in Theorem 6.1.1 was originally proved by J. Bourgain and L. Tzafriri
[55]. For related results and extensions, see the paper [97] and the books [96], [127, Section 8.4].
8   Here we use index g in Eg to indicate that this is an expectation “over (gi )”, i.e. conditional on
    (Xi ). Similarly, EX denotes the expectation over (Xi ).
                                                 Exercises                                        187

   The original Hanson-Wright inequality, somewhat weaker than Theorem 6.2.2, goes back to
[154, 350]. The modern version of Hanson-Wright inequality inequality (Theorem 6.2.2) and its
proof in Section 6.2 are from [292]. Several special cases appeared earlier in [127, Proposition 8.13]
for Bernoulli random variables, in [315, Lemma 2.5.1] for Gaussian random variables, and in
[31] for diagonal-free matrices.
   In [176], the dependence on K in Hanson-Wright inequality (Theorem 6.2.2) was improved,
assuming that Xi have variance one. For some extensions Hanson-Wright inequality, see [6, 34,
354, 8, 188, 145, 293, 159].
   Concentration for anisotropic random vectors (Exercise 6.13) and the bound on the distance
from a random vector to a subspace (Exercise 6.14) are from [292].
   Symmetrization Lemma 6.3.2 and its proof can be found e.g. in [210, Lemma 6.3], [127,
Section 8.2].
   Although the precise statement of Theorem 6.4.1 is difficult to locate in       existing literature,
                                                                                      √
it can be deduced, for example, from the inequalities in [324, 327]. The factor log n in Theo-
rem 6.4.1 can be improved to log1/4 n by combining a result of Y. Seginer [299, Theorem 3.1] with
symmetrization (Lemma 6.3.2); see [27, Corollary 4.7] for an alternative approach to Seginer
theorem. This improved factor is optimal as is demonstrated by the result of Exercise 6.29,
which is due to Y. Seginer [299, Theorem 3.2]. Moreover, for many classes of matrices the factor
log1/4 n can be removed completely; this happens, in particular, for matrices with i.i.d. entries
[299] and matrices with Gaussian entries [203]. Refer to [331, Section 4], [203, 25, 26, 59, 204]
for more elaborate results, whose goal is to describe the operator norm of a random matrix A
in terms of the variances of its entries.
   Theorem 6.5.1 on matrix completion and its proof are from [278, Section 2.5], although
versions of it may have appeared before. In particular, Keshavan, Montanari and Oh [182] showed
how to obtain a slightly better bound–one without the logarithmic factor–by “trimming” the
random matrix Y , where one removes the rows and columns of Y that have, say, twice more
many nonzero entries than expected. E. Candes and B. Recht [72] demonstrated that under
some additional incoherence assumptions, exact matrix completion (with zero error) is possible
with m ≍ rn log2 (n) randomly sampled entries. For further developments on matrix completion,
see [74, 282, 148, 92].
   The contraction principle (Theorem 6.6.1) is from [210, Section 4.2]; see also [210, Corol-
lary 3.17, Theorem 4.12] for different versions. Gaussian symmetrization (Lemma 6.6.2) can be
found in [210, inequality (4.9)]. While the logarithmic factor is in general needed there, it can
be removed if the normed space has non-trivial cotype, see [210, Proposition 9.14].
   In Exercise 6.4, the constant 4 can be improved to 2 for symmetric matrices [323].
   A version of Exercise 6.10 on the norm of anisotropic subgaussian random vectors was orig-
inally proved by D. Hsu, S. Kakade and T. Zhang [166]; they obtained sharp bounds working
with exact subgaussian norm (introduced in Exercise 2.40).
   The symmetry assumption in Exercise 6.22 is essential and cannot be skipped [135, Exam-
ple 2.8].

                                                   7

                               Random Processes


In this chapter, we turn our attention to random processes – collections of random
variables (Xt )t∈T , which may be dependent. In classical settings like Brownian
motion, t represents time, so T ⊂ R. But in high-dimensional probability, T can
be any abstract set. A key example is the canonical Gaussian process
                                        Xt = ⟨g, t⟩,         t ∈ T,
where T ⊂ Rn and g ∼ N (0, In ). We introduce it in Section 7.1.
   In Section 7.2, we explore powerful comparison inequalities for Gaussian pro-
cesses – Slepian, Sudakov-Fernique, and Gordon – using a new trick: Gaussian
interpolation. In Section 7.3, we use these tools to prove a sharp bound on the
operator norm of an m × n Gaussian random matrices.
   How does a Gaussian process (Xt )t∈T capture the geometry of T ? In Section 7.4,
we prove Sudakov inequality – a lower bound on the Gaussian width
                                         w(T ) = E sup⟨g, t⟩
                                                       t∈T

using covering numbers. (Upper bounds come later in Chapter 8.) Gaussian width
is a key concept linking probability with metric geometry; we take a closer look
at it in Section 7.5 and connect it to other ideas like effective dimension.
   In Section 7.6, we compute the size of a random projection of any bounded set
T ⊂ Rn . The crucial quantity that determines it is the Gaussian width.
   You will have plenty of chances to practice these ideas: prove symmetrization
and contraction inequalities for random processes (Exercises 7.2–7.4, 7.8), derive
the important min-max inequality for Gaussian processes – Gordon inequality
(Exercise 7.9), get sharp bounds for Gaussian matrices (Exercises 7.11 and 7.13),
compute the Gaussian width for the ℓp balls (Exercise 7.17), explore the nuclear
norm (Exercises 7.18 and 7.19), effective dimension (Exercises 7.21–7.22), ran-
dom projections of general sets (Exercises 7.25 and 7.26), and matrix sketching
(Exercise 7.27).
0   R. Vershynin. High-Dimensional Probability: An Introduction with Applications in Data Science.
    2nd ed. Cambridge University Press; 2026.

    This pre-publication version is free to view and download for personal use only. Not for
    re-distribution, re-sale or use in derivative works. ©Roman Vershynin 2026.
    https://www.math.uci.edu/~rvershyn/

196                                 Random Processes

                      7.1 Basic concepts and examples
Definition 7.1.1 (Random process). A random process is just a collection of
random variables (Xt )t∈T on the same probability space, which are indexed by
elements t of some set T .
  In some classical examples, t stands for time, in which case T is a subset of
R. But we primarily study processes in high-dimensional settings, where T is a
subset of Rn and where the analogy with time is lost.
Example 7.1.2 (Discrete time). If T = {1, . . . , n} then the random process
                                     (X1 , . . . , Xn )
can be identified with a random vector in Rn .
Example 7.1.3 (Random walks). If T = N, a discrete-time random process
(Xn )n∈N is simply a sequence of random variables. An important example is a
random walk defined as
                                       n
                                       X
                                 Xn :=   Zi ,
                                              i=1

where the increments Zi are independent, mean-zero random variables. See Fig-
ure 7.1 for illustration.

       20                                         20

       10                                         10

         0                                          0

       -10                                       -10

       -20                                       -20
             0   50    100    150      200              0   50   100   150    200


      Figure 7.1 A few trials of a random walk (left) and standard Brownian
      motion (right).


Example 7.1.4 (Brownian motion). The most classical continuous-time random
process is the standard Brownian motion (Xt )t≥0 , or the Wiener process. It can
be characterized as follows:
  (i) The process has continuous sample paths, i.e. the random function f (t) :=
      Xt is continuous almost surely;
 (ii) The increments are independent and satisfy Xt − Xs ∼ N (0, t − s) for all
      t ≥ s.
Figure 7.1 shows a few trials of the standard Brownian motion.
Example 7.1.5 (Random fields). When the index set T is a subset of Rn , a
random process (Xt )t∈T is sometimes called a spacial random process, or a random
field. For example, the water temperature Xt at the location on Earth that is
parametrized by t can be modeled as a spacial random process.
                                 7.1 Basic concepts and examples                                    197

                           7.1.1 Covariance and increments
In Section 3.2, we introduced the notion of the covariance matrix of a random
vector. We now define the covariance function of a random process (Xt )t∈T in
a similar manner. For simplicity, let us assume in this section that the random
process has zero mean, i.e.
                                     E Xt = 0       for all t ∈ T.
(The adjustments for the general case will be obvious.) The covariance function
of the process is defined as
                         Σ(t, s) := cov(Xt , Xs ) = E Xt Xs ,         t, s ∈ T.
The increments of the random process are defined as
                                                   1/2
            d(t, s) := ∥Xt − Xs ∥L2 = E(Xt − Xs )2      ,                    t, s ∈ T.            (7.1)
Example 7.1.6. The increments of the standard Brownian motion satisfy
                                 √
                        d(t, s) = t − s, t ≥ s
by definition. The increments of a random walk of Example 7.1.3 with E Zi2 = 1
behave similarly:
                                    √
                         d(n, m) = n − m, n ≥ m.
(Check!)
Remark 7.1.7 (The canonical metric). Even if the index T has no geometric
structure, the increments d(t, s) always define a metric on T , thus automatically
turning T into a metric space.1 However, as we see in Example 7.1.6, this metric
may not match the Euclidean distance on Rn .
Remark 7.1.8 (Covariance vs. increments). The covariance and the increments
contain roughly the same information about the random process. Increments can
be written using the covariance: just expand the square in (7.1) to see that
                             d(t, s)2 = Σ(t, t) − 2Σ(t, s) + Σ(s, s).
Vice versa, if the zero random variable 0 belongs to the process, you can also
recover the covariance from the increments (see Exercise 7.1).


                                  7.1.2 Gaussian processes
Definition 7.1.9 (Gaussian process). A random process (Xt )t∈T is called a
Gaussian process if, for any finite subset T0 ⊂ T , the random vector (Xt )t∈T0
has normal distribution.
              P            Equivalently, (Xt )t∈T is Gaussian if every finite linear
combination t∈T0 at Xt is a normal random variable. (To see why these two are
equivalent, recall Exercise 3.16.)
1   More precisely, d(t, s) is a pseudometric on T since the distance between two distinct points can be
    zero, i.e. d(t, s) = 0 does not necessarily imply t = s.
198                                       Random Processes

   The notion of Gaussian processes generalizes that of Gaussian random vectors
in Rn . A classical example of a Gaussian process is the standard Brownian motion.

Remark 7.1.10 (Distribution is determined by covariance, increments). The
distribution of a mean-zero Gaussian random vector in Rn is completely deter-
mined by its covariance matrix (recall Proposition 3.3.5). Then the same goes for
a mean-zero Gaussian process: its distribution is determined by the covariance
function Σ(t, s), or equivalently (due to Exercise 7.1) by the increments d(t, s),
assuming the zero variable is part of the process.
  Many tools we learned about random vectors can be applied to random pro-
cesses. For example, Gaussian concentration (Theorem 5.2.3) implies:
Theorem 7.1.11 (Concentration of Gaussian processes). Let (Xt )t∈T be a Gaus-
sian process with finite2 T . Then
                                              q
                     sup Xt − E sup Xt ≤ C sup Var(Xt ).
                           t∈T             t         ψ2      t∈T

    This is just a restatement of Exercise 5.9(b) – if you skipped it then, do it now!
  Let’s look at a broad class of Gaussian processes indexed by high-dimensional
sets T ⊂ Rn . Take a standard normal vector g ∼ N (0, In ) and define
                                        Xt := ⟨g, t⟩,      t ∈ T.                                  (7.2)
This gives us a Gaussian process (Xt )t∈T called the canonical Gaussian process.
The increments match the Euclidean distance:
                              ∥Xt − Xs ∥L2 = ∥t − s∥2 ,            t, s ∈ T.
(Check!)
  Actually, one can realize any Gaussian process as the canonical process (7.2).
This follows from a simple observation about Gaussian vectors:
Lemma 7.1.12 (Gaussian random vectors). Let X be a mean-zero Gaussian
random vector in Rn . Then there exist points t1 , . . . , tn ∈ Rn such that
                               dist            n
                           X = (⟨g, ti ⟩)i=1 ,        where g ∼ N (0, In ).
        dist
Here = means the equality of distributions.
Proof      If Σ denotes the covariance matrix of X, then, by (3.12), we have
                                 X ≡ Σ1/2 g        where g ∼ N (0, In ).
The entries of Σ1/2 g are ⟨ti , g⟩ where ti are the rows of Σ1/2 . Done!
  It follows that for any Gaussian process (Xs )s∈S , all finite-dimensional marginals
(Xs )s∈S0 , |S0 | = n can be represented as the canonical Gaussian process (7.2) in-
dexed in a certain subset T0 ⊂ Rn .

        7.2 Slepian, Sudakov-Fernique and Gordon inequalities
In many applications, it helps to have a uniform bound on a random process:
                                      E sup Xt =?
                                          t∈T

Remark 7.2.1 (Making T finite). To avoid measurability issues, let’s think of
E supt∈T Xt as shorthand for supT0 ⊂T E maxt∈T0 Xt where T0 runs over all finite
subsets. The general case usually follows by approximation.
  For some processes, this quantity can be computed exactly. For example, if
(Xt ) is a standard Brownian motion, the so-called reflection principle gives
                                  r
                                    2t0
                       E sup Xt =        for every t0 ≥ 0.
                         t≤t0        π
For general random processes – even Gaussian – the problem is nontrivial.
   The first general bound we prove is the Slepian comparison inequality for Gaus-
sian processes. It basically says: the faster the process grows (in terms of the
increments), the farther it gets.
Theorem 7.2.2 (Slepian inequality). Let (Xt )t∈T and (Yt )t∈T be two mean-zero
Gaussian processes. Assume that for all t, s ∈ T , we have
                E Xt2 = E Yt2     and     E(Xt − Xs )2 ≤ E(Yt − Ys )2 .      (7.3)
Then supt∈T Xt is stochastically dominated by supt∈T Yt : for every τ ∈ R, we have
                          n          o   n          o
                         P sup Xt ≥ τ ≤ P sup Yt ≥ τ .                       (7.4)
                            t∈T                   t∈T

Consequently,
                                  E sup Xt ≤ E sup Yt .                      (7.5)
                                    t∈T          t∈T

  We now prepare for the proof of Slepian inequality.


                        7.2.1 Gaussian interpolation
We will prove Slepian inequality using a trick called Gaussian interpolation. As-
sume that T is finite; then we can look at X = (Xt )t∈T and Y = (Yt )t∈T as
Gaussian random vectors in Rn where n = |T |. We may also assume that X and
Y are independent. (Why?)
  Define the Gaussian random vector Z(u) in Rn that continuously interpolates
between Z(0) = Y and Z(1) = X:
                             √        √
                    Z(u) := u X + 1 − u Y, u ∈ [0, 1].
Then the covariance matrix of Z(u) continuously interpolates linearly between
the covariance matrices of Y and X:
                        Σ(Z(u)) = u Σ(X) + (1 − u) Σ(Y ).
200                                 Random Processes

(Check!)
  For a given function f : Rn → R, let’s study how E f (Z(u)) changes as u
increases from 0 to 1. Of special interest to us is the function
                                   f (x) = 1{maxi xi <τ } .
We will be able to show that in this case, E f (Z(u)) increases in u. This would
imply the conclusion of Slepian inequality at once, since then
                                         n             o    n           o
       E f (Z(1)) ≥ E f (Z(0)), thus P max Xi < τ ≥ P max Yi < τ
                                                   i            i

as claimed.
  Now let us pass to a detailed argument. To develop Gaussian interpolation, let
us start with the following useful identity.
Lemma 7.2.3 (Gaussian integration by parts). Let X ∼ N (0, 1). Then for any
differentiable function f : R → R we have
                                E Xf (X) = E f ′ (X),

assuming both expectations exist and are finite.
Proof   Assume first that f has bounded support. Denoting the Gaussian density
by
                                           1   2
                                   p(x) = √ e−x /2 ,
                                           2π

                               ′

                               R                          R

                          Z

                           R

as claimed. The result can be extended to general functions by an approximation
argument. The lemma is proved.
  By rescaling, we can extend Gaussian integration by parts for X ∼ N (0, σ 2 ):
                               E Xf (X) = σ 2 E f ′ (X).

(Just write X = σZ for Z ∼ N (0, 1) and apply Lemma 7.2.3.) We can also
extend it to high dimensions:
Lemma 7.2.4 (Multivariate Gaussian integration by parts). Let X ∼ N (0, Σ).
Then for any differentiable function f : Rn → R we have
                            E Xf (X) = Σ · E ∇f (X),

                      7.2.2 Proof of Slepian inequality
We are ready to establish a preliminary, functional form Slepian inequality.
Lemma 7.2.6 (Slepian inequality, functional form). Consider two mean-zero
Gaussian random vectors X and Y in Rn . Assume that for all i, j = 1, . . . , n, we
have
                E Xi2 = E Yi2    and   E(Xi − Xj )2 ≤ E(Yi − Yj )2 .
Consider a twice-differentiable function f : Rn → R such that
                             ∂2f
                                    ≥0     for all i ̸= j.
                            ∂xi ∂xj
Then
                                 E f (X) ≥ E f (Y ),
assuming both expectations exist and are finite.
Proof The assumptions imply that the entries of the covariance matrices ΣX
and ΣY of X and Y satisfy
                           ΣX     Y
                            ii = Σii    and   ΣX     Y
                                               ij ≥ Σij .

for all i, j = 1, . . . , n. We can assume that X and Y are independent. (Why?)
Apply Lemma 7.2.5 and using our assumptions, we conclude that
                                  d
                                    E f (Z(u)) ≥ 0,
                                 du
so E f (Z(u)) increases in u. Then E f (Z(1)) = E f (X) is at least as large as
E f (Z(0)) = E f (Y ). This completes the proof.
  Now we are ready to prove Slepian inequality, Theorem 7.2.2. Let us state and
prove it in the equivalent form for Gaussian random vectors.
Theorem 7.2.7 (Slepian inequality). Let X and Y be Gaussian random vectors
as in Lemma 7.2.6. Then for every τ ≥ 0 we have
                                       
                     P max Xi ≥ τ ≤ P max Yi ≥ τ .
                           i≤n                 i≤n

Consequently,
                                 E max Xi ≤ E max Yi .
                                   i≤n             i≤n

Proof Let h : R → [0, 1] be a twice-differentiable, non-increasing approximation
to the indicator function of the interval (−∞, τ ):
                                    h(x) ≈ 1(−∞,τ ) ,
like in Figure 7.2. Define the function f : Rn → R by




     Figure 7.2 The function h(x) is a smooth, non-increasing approximation to
     the indicator function 1(−∞,τ ) .


                               f (x) = h(x1 ) · · · h(xn ).
Then f (x) is an approximation to the indicator function
                                  f (x) ≈ 1{maxi xi <τ } .
We are looking to apply the functional form of Slepian inequality, Lemma 7.2.6,
for f (x). To check the assumptions of this result, note that for i ̸= j we have
                        ∂2f                           Y

                       ∂xi ∂xj                      k̸∈{i,j}

The first two factors are non-positive and the others are nonnegative by the

  It follows that
                                  E f (X) ≥ E f (Y ).

                       n          o   n          o
                      P max Xi < τ ≥ P max Yi < τ .

integrated tail formula in Exercise 1.15(b) (check!)


            7.2.3 Sudakov-Fernique and Gordon inequalities
Slepian inequality has two assumptions on the processes (Xt ) and (Yt ) in (7.3):
the equality of variances and the dominance of increments. We now remove the
assumption on the equality of variances, and still be able to obtain (7.5).
204                                         Random Processes

Theorem 7.2.8 (Sudakov-Fernique inequality). Let (Xt )t∈T and (Yt )t∈T be two
mean-zero Gaussian processes. Assume that for all t, s ∈ T , we have
                                     E(Xt − Xs )2 ≤ E(Yt − Ys )2 .
Then
                                         E sup Xt ≤ E sup Yt .
                                            t∈T           t∈T

Proof It is enough to prove this theorem for Gaussian random vectors X and Y
in Rn , just like we did for Slepian inequality in Theorem 7.2.7. We again deduce
the result from Gaussian Interpolation Lemma 7.2.5. But this time, instead of
choosing f (x) that approximates the indicator function of {maxi xi < τ }, we
want f (x) to approximate maxi xi .
   To this end, let β > 0 be a parameter and define the function4
                                                     n
                                               1    X
                                       f (x) := log     eβxi .                                   (7.12)
                                               β    i=1

A quick check shows that
                                     f (x) → max xi       as β → ∞.
                                               i≤n

(Do this!) Substituting f (x) into the Gaussian interpolation formula (7.9) and
simplifying the expression shows that
                               d
                                 E f (Z(u)) ≤ 0        for all u ∈ [0, 1].
                              du
(Work through this step carefully in Exercise 7.7!) Then we can finish the proof
just like in Slepian inequality.
  Some applications call for min-max bounds on random processes. For example,
by (4.14), the smallest singular value of an m × n Gaussian matrix A with all
N (0, 1) i.i.d. entries can be written as
                         sn (A) = min
                                   n−1
                                       ∥Au∥2 = min
                                                n−1
                                                    max
                                                     m−1
                                                         Xuv ,
                                      u∈S                 u∈S    v∈S

where Xuv = ⟨Au, v⟩ are normal random variables. A handy tool for this kind of
problems is Gordon inequality, which extends Slepian and Sudakov-Fernique to
the min-max setting:
Theorem 7.2.9 (Gordon inequality). Let (Xut )u∈U, t∈T and Y = (Yut )u∈U, t∈T be
two mean-zero Gaussian processes indexed by pairs of points (u, t) in a product
set U × T . Assume that
                E(Xut − Xus )2 ≤ E(Yut − Yus )2            for all u, t, s;
                                 2                    2
                E(Xut − Xvs ) ≥ E(Yut − Yvs )              for all u ̸= v and all t, s.
4   The motivation for considering this form of f (x) comes from statistical mechanics, where the right
    side of (7.12) can be interpreted as a log-partition function and β as the inverse temperature.
                7.3 Application: sharp bounds for Gaussian matrices                       205

Then, for every τ ≥ 0,
                   n               o   n               o
                  P inf sup Xut ≥ τ ≤ P inf sup Yut ≥ τ ,
                          u∈U t∈T                         u∈U t∈T

and so, by the integrated tail formula,
                                 E inf sup Xut ≤ E inf sup Yut .
                                   u∈U t∈T               u∈U t∈T

  Try yourself to prove Gordon inequality under an additional assumption of
equal variances (Exercise 7.9). Also try your hand at a Gaussian version of Tala-
grand contraction principle (Exercise 7.8).


         7.3 Application: sharp bounds for Gaussian matrices
Let’s apply the Gaussian comparison inequalities we just proved to random ma-
trices. In Section 4.6, we studied m × n random matrices A with independent
subgaussian rows. We used the ε-net argument to bound the expected operator
norm of A like this:
                                      √       √
                              E∥A∥ ≤ m + C n
where C is a constant (see Exercise 4.41). Now, using the Sudakov-Fernique
inequality, we will tighten this bound for Gaussian random matrices, proving it
with sharp constant C = 1.
Theorem 7.3.1 (Norms of Gaussian random matrices). Let A be an m×n matrix
with independent N (0, 1) entries. Then
                                       √ √
                               E∥A∥ ≤ m + n.
Proof   Let’s write the norm of A as a supremum of a Gaussian process: by (4.9),
                        ∥A∥ =           max            ⟨Au, v⟩ = max Xuv
                                   u∈S n−1 , v∈S m−1               (u,v)∈T

              n−1        m−1
where T = S         ×S          and
                                   Xuv := ⟨Au, v⟩ ∼ N (0, 1).
  To apply Sudakov-Fernique comparison inequality (Theorem 7.2.8), let us com-
pute the increments of the process (Xuv ). For any (u, v), (w, z) ∈ T , we have
                                               X                       2
                                         2
  E(Xuv − Xwz )2 = E (⟨Au, v⟩ − ⟨Aw, z⟩) = E        Aij (uj vi − wj zi )
                                                               i,j
                        X
                                               2
                    =          (uj vi − wj zi )    (by independence, mean 0, variance 1)
                         i,j

                    = ∥uv T − wz T ∥2F
                    ≤ ∥u − w∥22 + ∥v − z∥22              (check this in Exercise 7.10).
  Let’s define a simpler Gaussian process (Yuv ) with similar increments:
                               Yuv := ⟨g, u⟩ + ⟨h, v⟩,       (u, v) ∈ T,
206                                Random Processes

where g ∼ N (0, In ) and h ∼ N (0, Im ) are independent Gaussian vectors. The
increments of this process are
                                                     2
      E(Yuv − Ywz )2 = E (⟨g, u − w⟩ + ⟨h, v − z⟩)
                     = E⟨g, u − w⟩2 + E⟨h, v − z⟩2 (by independence, mean 0)
                     = ∥u − w∥22 + ∥v − z∥22 (since g, h are standard normal).
  Comparing the increments of the two processes, we see that
             E(Xuv − Xwz )2 ≤ E(Yuv − Ywz )2         for all (u, v), (w, z) ∈ T,
as required in Sudakov-Fernique inequality. Applying Theorem 7.2.8, we obtain
              E ∥A∥ = E sup Xuv ≤ E sup Yuv
                         (u,v)∈T          (u,v)∈T

                     ≤ (E ∥g∥22 )1/2 + (E ∥h∥22 )1/2 (recall Exercise 1.11)

                     = n + m (see Proposition 3.2.1(b)).
  Theorem 7.3.1 only gives an expectation bound, but we can boost it to a high-
probability bound using the concentration tools from Section 5.2:
Corollary 7.3.2 (Norms of Gaussian random matrices: tails). Let A be an m×n
matrix with independent N (0, 1) entries. Then for every t ≥ 0, we have
                              √      √
                   P ∥A∥ ≥ m + n + t ≤ 2 exp(−ct2 ).
                     

Proof Let’s combine the expectation bound (Theorem 7.3.1) with Gaussian
concentration (Theorem 5.2.3). Think of A as a long random vector in Rm×n
by concatenating the rows. This makes A a standard normal random vector:
A ∼ N (0, Inm ). Consider the function
                                     f (A) := ∥A∥
that maps the vectorized matrix to the matrix’s operator norm. Since the operator
norm is bounded by Frobenius norm, and the Frobenius norm is just the Euclidean
norm on Rm×n , f is a Lipschitz function on Rm×n with Lipschitz norm is bounded
by 1. (Why?) Then Theorem 5.2.3 yields
                         P{∥A∥ ≥ E∥A∥ + t} ≤ 2 exp(−ct2 ).
The bound on E∥A∥ from Theorem 7.3.1 completes the proof.
  Now try Exercise 7.11 to prove that a symmetric Gaussian matrix satisfies
                                          √
                                E∥A∥ ≤ 2 n,
and Exercise 7.13 to show that the smallest singular value of an m × n Gaussian
matrix A satisfies
                                       √       √
                             E sn (A) ≥ m − n.
                                        7.4 Sudakov inequality                 207

                                    7.4 Sudakov inequality
Let’s go back to general mean-zero Gaussian processes (Xt )t∈T on any index set
T . As we saw earlier (Remark 7.1.7), the increments
                                                           1/2
                    d(t, s) := ∥Xt − Xs ∥L2 = E(Xt − Xs )2               (7.13)
define a metric on T , called the canonical metric. This metric determines the co-
variance function Σ(t, s), which in turn determines the distribution of the process
(Xt )t∈T (recall Remark 7.1.10). So, in theory, we can answer any question about
the distribution of the process just by understanding the geometry of the metric
space (T, d) – in other words, we can study probability via geometry.
  Here is a big specific question: how can we estimate
                                                 E sup Xt                   (7.14)
                                                    t∈T

in terms of the geometry of (T, d)? This is a hard problem, which we begin to
study now and continue in Chapter 8.
  We will start with a lower bound on (7.14) in terms of metric entropy, which
was in introduced Section 4.2. Recall that for any ε > 0, the covering number
                                                 N (T, d, ε)
is the smallest cardinality of an ε-net of T in the metric d, or equivalently the
smallest number5 of closed balls of radius ε whose union covers T . The logarithm
of the covering number, log2 N (T, d, ε), is called the metric entropy of T .
Theorem 7.4.1 (Sudakov inequality). Let (Xt )t∈T be a mean-zero Gaussian
process. Then, for any ε ≥ 0, we have
                                      q
                         E sup Xt ≥ cε log N (T, d, ε).
                                     t∈T

where d is the canonical metric defined in (7.13).
Proof Let us deduce this result from Sudakov-Fernique comparison inequality
(Theorem 7.2.8). Assume that
                                            N (T, d, ε) =: N
is finite; you will handle the infinite case in Exercise 7.14. Let N be a maximal
ε-separated subset of T . Then N is an ε-net of T (recall Lemma 4.2.6), and thus
                                                 |N | ≥ N.
Restricting the process to N , we see that it suffices to show that
                                            p
                              E sup Xt ≥ cε log N .
                                           t∈N

5   If T does not have a finite ε-net, we set N (T, d, ε) = ∞.
208                                   Random Processes

  Let’s do it by comparing (Xt )t∈N to a simpler Gaussian process (Yt )t∈N , defined
as follows:
               ε
        Yt := √ gt , where gt are independent N (0, 1) random variables.
                2
To use Sudakov-Fernique comparison inequality (Theorem 7.2.8), we need to
compare the increments of the two processes. Fix two different points t, s ∈ N .
By definition, we have
                               E(Xt − Xs )2 = d(t, s)2 ≥ ε2
while
                                        ε2
                         E(Yt − Ys )2 =    E(gt − gs )2 = ε2 .
                                        2
(In the last line, we use that gt − gs ∼ N (0, 2).) This implies that
                   E(Xt − Xs )2 ≥ E(Yt − Ys )2      for all t, s ∈ N .
  Applying Theorem 7.2.8, we obtain
                                        ε             p
                 E sup Xt ≥ E sup Yt = √ E max gt ≥ cε log N .
                   t∈N        t∈N        2 t∈N
In the last step, we used
                       √ that the expected maximum of N i.i.d. N (0, 1) random
variables is at least c log N , see Exercise 2.38(b). The proof is complete.


              7.4.1 Application for covering numbers in Rn
Sudakov inequality can be used to bound the covering numbers of an arbitrary
set T ⊂ Rn :
Corollary 7.4.2 (Sudakov inequality in Rn ). Let T ⊂ Rn . Then, for any ε > 0,
we have
                                       q
                       E sup⟨g, t⟩ ≥ cε log N (T, ε).
                                t∈T

Here N (T, ε) is the smallest number of Euclidean balls with radius ε and centers
in T that cover T , just like in Section 4.2.1.
Proof Consider the canonical Gaussian process Xt := ⟨g, t⟩ where g ∼ N (0, In ).
As we noted in Section 7.1.2, the canonical distance for this process is the Eu-
clidean distance in Rn , i.e. d(t, s) = ∥Xt − Xs ∥L2 = ∥t − s∥2 for any t, s ∈ T . Then
the corollary follows from Sudakov inequality (Theorem 7.4.1).
  In Exercise 8.5, you will show that Corollary 7.4.2 is sharp up to a log factor:
                                               q
                     E sup⟨g, t⟩ ≤ C log(n) · ε log N (T, ε).
                         t∈T

  For a quick application of Sudakov inequality, let’s (roughly) re-derive the
bound on covering numbers of polytopes in Rn from Corollary 0.0.3:
                                         7.5 Gaussian width                                         209

Corollary 7.4.3 (Covering numbers of polytopes). Let P be a polytope in Rn
with N vertices, contained in the unit Euclidean ball. Then, for every ε > 0 we
have
                                                             2
                                         N (P, ε) ≤ N C/ε .
Proof     If x1 , . . . , xN are the vertices of P , then
                                                                   p
                            E sup⟨g, t⟩ ≤ E sup⟨g, xi ⟩ ≤ C            log N .                  (7.15)
                               t∈P             i≤N

The first bound follows from the maximal principle (Exercise 1.4): since P lies the
convex hull of its vertices, for each fixed g the linear (and thus convex) function
t 7→ ⟨g, t⟩ attains its maximum at a vertex. The second bound in (7.15) is due to
the maximal inequality (2.22), since ⟨g, x⟩ ∼ N (0, ∥x∥22 ) and ∥x∥2 ≤ 1. Substitute
this into Corollary 7.4.2 and simplify to complete the proof.


                                      7.5 Gaussian width
In Section 7.4.1, we saw an important quantity associated with any set T ⊂
Rn : the size of the canonical Gaussian process on T . It shows up a lot in high-
dimensional probability, so let’s give it a name and look at its basic properties.
Definition 7.5.1. The Gaussian width of a subset T ⊂ Rn is defined as
                         w(T ) := E sup⟨g, x⟩        where       g ∼ N (0, In ).
                                      x∈T

  You can think of Gaussian width as a fundamental geometric measure of a set
T ⊂ Rn , like volume or surface area.
Proposition 7.5.2 (Simple properties of Gaussian width).
 (a) (Finiteness) w(T ) is finite if and only if T is bounded.
 (b) (Invariance) w(U T + y) = w(T ) for any orthogonal matrix U and vector y.
 (c) (Convex hulls) w(conv(T )) = w(T ).
 (d) (Minkowski addition and scaling)6 w(T + S) = w(T ) + w(S) and w(aT ) =
     |a| w(T ) for any T, S ⊂ Rn and a ∈ R.
 (e) (Symmetry):
                                    1           1
                             w(T ) = w(T − T ) = E sup ⟨g, x − y⟩.
                                    2           2 x,y∈T
 (f ) (Width and diameter):7
                                                     √
                               1                       n
                              √ · diam(T ) ≤ w(T ) ≤     · diam(T ).
                               2π                     2
 (g) (Linear maps) For any m × n matrix A, we have w(AT ) ≤ ∥A∥ w(T ).
6   Recall Definition 4.2.9 of Minkowski sum and difference of two sets: T ± S = {t ± s : t ∈ T, s ∈ S}.
7   Recall that the diameter of a set T ⊂ Rn is defined as diam(T ) := sup{∥x − y∥2 : x, y ∈ T }.
210                                 Random Processes

Proof Let’s just prove part (f) and leave the rest for you in Exercise 7.15.
  For the lower bound, fix any x, y ∈ T . Since both x − y and y − x are in T − T ,
property (e) gives
                                                                  r
            1                                 1                 1 2
   w(T ) ≥ E max ⟨x − y, g⟩, ⟨y − x, g⟩ = E|⟨x − y, g⟩| =            ∥x − y∥2 .
            2                                  2                 2 π
                                                                        p
The last equality holds since ⟨x − y, g⟩ ∼ N (0, ∥x − y∥22 ) and E|X| = 2/π for
X ∼ N (0, 1). (Check!) Taking the supremum over all x, y ∈ T gives the result.
  For the upper bound in (f), use property (e) again to get
            1                   1                     1
      w(T ) = E sup ⟨g, x − y⟩ ≤ E sup ∥g∥2 ∥x − y∥2 ≤ E∥g∥2 · diam(T ).
            2 x,y∈T             2 x,y∈T               2
                            √
Since E∥g∥2 ≤ (E∥g∥22 )1/2 = n, we are done.
Remark 7.5.3 (Width√     and diameter). Both upper and lower bounds in (f) are
optimal and the O( n) gap between them cannot be improved (see Exercise 7.16).
So, diameter is not a great way to capture Gaussian width.


                      7.5.1 Geometric meaning of width
Gaussian width has a nice geometric meaning: it’s about how wide the set T ⊂ Rn
looks in random directions. The width of T in the direction θ ∈ S n−1 is the width
of the smallest slab (between parallel hyperplanes orthogonal to θ) that contains
T (see Figure 7.3), which can be expressed as supx,y∈T ⟨θ, x − y⟩ (check)! If we
average the width over all unit directions θ, we get
                          E sup ⟨θ, x − y⟩ = E sup ⟨θ, z⟩.                         (7.16)
                            x,y∈T               z∈T −T




       Figure 7.3 The width of a set T ⊂ Rn in the direction of a unit vector θ.


Definition 7.5.4 (Spherical width). The spherical width of a set T ⊂ Rn is
                  ws (T ) := E sup⟨θ, x⟩   where   θ ∼ Unif(S n−1 ).
                              x∈T

  The only difference between the Gaussian and spherical widths is in the random
vectors we average over: g ∼ N (0,√In ) versus θ ∼ Unif(S n−1 ). Both are rotation
invariant, but g is approximately n times longer than θ. Thus:
                                7.5 Gaussian width                                211

Lemma√ 7.5.5 (Gaussian vs. spherical widths). The Gaussian width is approxi-
mately n times the spherical width:
                 √       C                   √
                     n − √ ws (T ) ≤ w(T ) ≤ n ws (T ).
                           n
Proof Express the Gaussian vector g through its length and direction: g = rθ,
where r = ∥g∥2 and θ = g/∥g∥2 . Now, θ ∼ Unif(S n−1 ) is independent of r (see
Exercise 3.22). Thus
            w(T ) = E sup⟨rθ, x⟩ = E[r] · E sup⟨θ, x⟩ = E∥g∥2 · ws (T ).
                       x∈T                  x∈T
                                                                              √
It remains
    √      to use concentration
                   √            of the norm (see Exercise 3.2), which gives       n−
C/ n ≤ E∥g∥2 ≤ n, and finishes the proof.

To prove this, we can assume that diam(T ) = 1/2 (by rescaling), and that T lies
in the unit Euclidean ball (by translation). Then argue as in (7.15) (do it!)
212                                   Random Processes

Remark 7.5.10 (Surprising behavior of width in high dimensions). As we can
                                                                       n
see from Examples 7.5.6–7.5.8, the Gaussian width of the cube B∞         √is roughly
(up to a constant factor) the same as that of its circumscribed ball nB2n . But
for the cross-polytope B1n , the width is roughly (up to a log factor) like that of
its inscribed ball √1n B2n , which is tiny! Why?
               n
   The cube B∞    has so many vertices (2n ) that in most directions it sticks out to
roughly the circumscribed ball, which drives the width. But the cross-polytope
B1n only has 2n vertices, so a random direction g ∼ N (0, In ) likely to be far from
all of them. The width is not driven by those lonely 2n “spikes” – it’s driven by
the “bulk”, which is roughly the inscribed ball.
   Figure 7.4a shows Milman’s hyperbolic sketch of B1n , highlighting how the bulk
(the inscribed ball) dominates since set has few vertices (spikes). You can make
similar sketches for general convex sets, too (Figure 7.4b) – they are great for
building high-dimensional intuition, even if you lose convexity in the picture.
  For more practice, try Exercise 7.17 to compute the Gaussian width of any
ℓp ball, covering all three examples above at once – the ball (p = 2), the cube
(p = ∞), and the cross-polytope (p = 1). Also work through Exercises 7.18–7.19
to compute the Gaussian width of the set of n × n matrices with operator norm
at most 1.




             (a) The octahedron B1n                      (b) General convex set


      Figure 7.4 V. Milman’s hyperbolic sketch of high-dimensional convex sets




           7.5.3 Gaussian complexity and effective dimension
There are a couple of helpful cousins of the Gaussian width w(T ). Normally, we
would take the expected max of ⟨g, t⟩, but sometimes it’s easier to work with L1
or L2 averages:
                                                                       1/2
    w(T ) = E sup⟨g, x⟩, γ(T ) := E sup|⟨g, x⟩|, h(T ) := E sup⟨g, x⟩2       ,
               x∈T                        x∈T                           x∈T

where g ∼ N (0, In ). We call γ(T ) the Gaussian complexity of T . Clearly,
                                 w(T ) ≤ γ(T ) ≤ h(T ),
                                          7.5 Gaussian width                    213

and the reverse bounds are basically true too:

Lemma 7.5.11 (Almost equivalent versions of Gaussian width). For any bounded
set T ⊂ Rn , we have:8
 (a) γ(T − T ) = 2w(T ).
 (b) h(T ) ≍ γ(T ) ≍ w(T ) + ∥y∥2 for any point y ∈ T .
In particular, if T contains the origin, all three versions are equivalent:

                                         h(T ) ≍ γ(T ) ≍ w(T ).

Proof (a) follows from Proposition 7.5.2(e), since T − T is origin-symmetric.
  (b) Let’s just prove the first part and leave the equivalence γ(T ) ≍ w(T ) +∥y∥2
for you to prove in Exercise 7.20. We trivially have γ(T ) ≤ h(T ). For the reverse,
look at the function z 7→ supx∈T |⟨z, x⟩| on Rn . Its Lipschitz norm is bounded by
the radius supx∈T ∥x∥2 = r(T ) (check!). Then, by Gaussian concentration (5.6),

                                   sup|⟨g, x⟩| − γ(T ) ψ2 ≲ r(T ).
                                   x∈T

So, by triangle inequality and Proposition 2.6.6(ii), we get

            h(T ) = sup|⟨g, x⟩| L2 ≲ sup|⟨g, x⟩| ψ2 ≲ γ(T ) + r(T ) ≲ γ(T ).
                        x∈T                   x∈T

The last step used the fact that γ(T ) ≳ r(T ), which comes from the second part
of (b) – just take the supremum over y ∈ T .

   The Gaussian width helps us define a robust version of the notion of dimension.
The usual linear-algebraic dimension of a set T ⊂ Rn , which is the dimension of
the smallest affine space containing it, can change a lot with tiny perturbations
of T . Here is a more robust alternative:

Definition 7.5.12 (Effective dimension). The effective dimension of a bounded
set T ⊂ Rn is
                               h(T − T )2     w(T )2
                      d(T ) :=            ≍           .
                               diam(T )2    diam(T )2
  The equivalence follows from Lemma 7.5.11. The effective dimension is bounded
by the linear-algebraic one:
                                           d(T ) ≤ dim(T ),

with equality when T is a Euclidean ball in some subspace (see Exercise 7.21).
Unlike the usual dimension, the effective one is stable – small perturbations to T
only slightly change its width and diameter.
   To get some practice with effective dimension, compute it for ellipsoids (Exer-
cise 7.23) and bound it for general finite sets (Exercise 7.22).
8   Here, as usual, the notation ≍ hides positive asbsolute constant factors.
214                               Random Processes

                7.6 Application: random projections of sets
What happens if we project a set T ⊂ Rn onto a random m-dimensional subspace
in Rn (picked uniformly from the Grassmanian Gn,m ), like in Figure 5.2? In
practice, we might see T as data and P as a way of dimension reduction, like in
Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma. What can we say about the size (diameter) of the
projected set P T ?
  For a finite set T , the Johnson-Lindenstrauss Lemma (Theorem 5.3.1) says
that if m ≳ log|T |, the random projection P acts essentially
                                                        p as a scaling of T : it
shrinks all distances between points in T by a factor ≈ m/n. In particular,
                                         r
                                           m
                            diam(P T ) ≈     diam(T ).                  (7.20)
                                           n
   But if the cardinality of T is too large or infinite, (7.20) may fail. For instance,
if T = B2n is a Euclidean ball, no projection can shrink its size:
                               diam(P T ) = diam(T ).                           (7.21)
  What about general sets T ? The next result states that a random projection
shrinks T as in (7.20), but it cannot shrink it beyond the spherical width ws (T ):
Theorem 7.6.1 (Sizes of random projections of sets). Let T ⊂ Rn be a bounded
set, and P be the orthogonal projection in Rn onto a random m-dimensional
subspace E ∼ Unif(Gn,m ). Then
                                            r
                                              m
                   E diam(P T ) ≍ ws (T ) +     diam(T ),
                                              n
where the notation ≍ hides positive absolute constant factors.
Proof Let’s prove the upper bound and leave the lower bound to you (see Ex-
ercise 7.26).
   Step 1: Change the model. Let’s switch the view just like in the proof of
Proposition 5.3.2. A random subspace E ⊂ Rn can be obtained by randomly
rotating some fixed subspace, such as Rm . But instead of fixing T and randomly
rotating Rm , we can fix E = Rm and randomly rotate T . A random rotation
of a vector x ∈ T is U x where U ∼ Unif(O(n)) is a random orthogonal matrix.
Projecting U x onto E = Rm means keeping the first m coordinates, i.e. Qx where
Q is the m × n matrix consisting of the first m columns of U . So, we can work
with Q instead of P .
   Step 2: Approximation. Without loss of generality, diam(T ) ≤ 1. (Why?)
We need to bound
                diam(QT ) = sup ∥Qx∥2 = sup             max
                                                         m−1
                                                             ⟨Qx, z⟩.
                               x∈T −T           x∈T −T z∈S

We will proceed with an ε-net argument as in the proof of Theorem 4.4.3. Choose
an (1/2)-net N of the sphere S m−1 so that
                                        |N | ≤ 5m

using Corollary 4.2.11. We can replace the supremum over the sphere S m−1 by
the supremum over the net N paying a factor 2:
            diam(QT ) ≤ 2 sup max⟨Qx, z⟩ = 2 max sup ⟨QT z, x⟩.             (7.22)
                             x∈T −T z∈N             z∈N x∈T −T

                                    sup ⟨QT z, x⟩                           (7.23)

  Step 3: Concentration. So, let’s fix z ∈ N . By construction, QT z is uni-

                    E sup ⟨QT z, x⟩ = ws (T − T ) = 2ws (T ).

Remark 7.6.2 (Phase transition). Let’s get more insight from Theorem 7.6.1.
Since the sum of two terms is equivalent to maximum (up to factor 2), we can
write:                                      r

                  diam(P T ) ≍ max ws (T ),      diam(T ) .

                            √

                     m=               2

                                (p

(see Figure 7.5). That is, as we decrease p   the dimension m of the random pro-

and the diameter stays near the spherical width ws (T ) as in (7.21). This is because
conv(P T ) looks like a ball of radius ws (T ), as we will see in Section 9.7.2.

                                         7.7 Notes
Slepian inequality (Theorem 7.2.2) is originally due to D. Slepian [304, 305]; modern proofs can
be found e.g. in [210, Corollary 3.12], [11, Section 2.2], [330, Section 6.1], [169], [180].
   Sudakov-Fernique inequality (Theorem 7.2.8) is attributed to V. N. Sudakov [309, 310] and
X. Fernique [124]. Our presentation of the proofs of Slepian and Sudakov-Fernique inequalities
in Section 7.2 is based on the approach of J.-P. Kahane [180] and a smoothing argument of
S. Chatterjee (see [11, Section 2.2]), and it follows [330, Section 6.1].
   The relevance of comparison inequalities to random matrix theory was noticed by S. Szarek.
The applications in Section 7.3 can derived from the work of Y. Gordon [140]. Our presentation
there follows the argument in [94, Section II.c], reproduced also in [340, Section 5.3.1].
   Sudakov inequality (Theorem 7.4.1) was originally proved by V. N. Sudakov. Our presentation
follows [210, Theorem 3.18]; see [21, Section 4.2] for an alternative proof via duality.
   Gaussian width, introduced in Section 7.5, originates in geometric functional analysis and
asymptotic convex geometry [21, 246]. Starting from [290], the role of Gaussian width was
recognized in applications to signal processing and high-dimensional statistics. Milman’s “hy-
perbolic sketch” of a high-dimensional convex body discussed in Remark 7.5.10 is from [241];
for more on this, see the preface of [21] and [23].
   The effective dimension introduced in Section 7.5.3 has some variants in the literature, in-
cluding the statistical dimension for convex cones [228, 18, 267].
   Theorem 7.6.1 on the diameters of random projections of sets is due to to V. Milman [245],
see also [21, Proposition 5.7.1].
   Talagrand contraction principle (Exercise 7.3) can be found in [210, Corollary 3.17], where
one can find a more general result (with a convex and increasing function of the supremum).
                                             Exercises                                              217

Exercise 7.3 is adapted from [330, Exercise 7.4]. A more general version of Gaussian contraction
inequality in Exercise 7.8 can be found in [210, Corollary 3.17].
   Gordon inequality (Theorem 7.2.9) and its extensions can be found in [140, 141, 144, 180].
   Exercise 7.11 on the norms of symmetric Gaussian matrices and Exercise 7.13(a) on the
smallest singular values of Gaussian matrices are from [94, Section II.c].
   Exercise 7.18 introduces the nuclear norm – one of the Schatten norms, which are the ℓp
norms of the matrix’s singular values. Special cases include the nuclear norm (p = 1), Frobenius
norm (p = 2), and operator norm (p = ∞). The duality extends naturally to all Schatten norms,
and can be deduced von Neumann trace inequality.

                                                   8

                                          Chaining


This chapter covers some of the central methods for bounding random processes
(Xt )t∈T . In Section 8.1, we introduce the chaining technique and use it to bound
E supt∈T Xt in terms of covering numbers of T , a result known as Dudley inequal-
ity. We illustrate it in Section 8.2 with an application to Monte Carlo integration
and empirical processes.
   Then in Section 8.3 we introduce the VC theory, which gives combinatorial
rather than metric insights into random processes. We apply it for statistical
learning theory in Section 8.4.
   In Section 8.5, we refine the chaining method to generic chaining and get
optimal, two-sided bounds on random processes, in terms of Talagrand’s γ2 (T )
functional (we only prove an the upper bound here). A useful consequence is
Talagrand comparison inequality, which generalizes Sudakov-Fernique inequality
for subgaussian processes.
   Finally, in Section 8.6, we use Talagrand comparison inequality to derive Chevet
inequality, a handy tool for random bilinear forms over general sets.
   With everything you’ve learned, some exercises now feel like fun mini research
results – Lipschitz law of large numbers in higher dimensions (Exercise 8.10),
one-bit quantization (Exercise 8.26), the small ball method with applications
to heavy-tailed random matrices (Exercise 8.27), and p → q norms of random
matrices (Exercise 8.41), just to mention a few!



                                    8.1 Dudley inequality
For a general Gaussian process (Xt )t∈T , Sudakov inequality (Theorem 7.4.1) gives
a lower bound on

                                               E sup Xt
                                                  t∈T


0   R. Vershynin. High-Dimensional Probability: An Introduction with Applications in Data Science.
    2nd ed. Cambridge University Press; 2026.

    This pre-publication version is free to view and download for personal use only. Not for

                                                  222
                                  8.1 Dudley inequality                       223

in terms of the metric entropy of T . Now we will go for an upper bound. And
instead of sticking just to Gaussian processes, we will take it further and handle
more general, subgaussian ones.
Definition 8.1.1 (Subgaussian increments). A random process (Xt )t∈T on a
metric space (T, d) has subgaussian increments if there exists K ≥ 0 such that
                    ∥Xt − Xs ∥ψ2 ≤ Kd(t, s)       for all t, s ∈ T.          (8.1)
Example 8.1.2 (Gaussian processes). Let (Xt )t∈T be a Gaussian process on
some set T . It naturally defines a canonical metric on T :
                        d(t, s) := ∥Xt − Xs ∥L2 ,    t, s ∈ T,
as we explained in (7.1). With respect to this metric, (Xt )t∈T clearly had sub-
gaussian increments, with some absolute constant K.
  Here is another (trivial) example: any random process can be made to have
subgaussian increments by defining the metric as d(t, s) := ∥Xt − Xs ∥ψ2 .
  Now we give a bound on a general subgaussian random process (Xt )t∈T in
terms of the metric entropy log N (T, d, ε):
Theorem 8.1.3 (Dudley integral inequality). Let (Xt )t∈T be a mean-zero random
process on a metric space (T, d) with subgaussian increments as in (8.1). Then
                                     Z ∞q
                     E sup Xt ≤ CK         log N (T, d, ε) dε.
                       t∈T               0

  Before we prove Dudley inequality, let’s compare it with Sudakov inequality
(Theorem 7.4.1), which for Gaussian processes says:
                                        q
                      E sup Xt ≥ c sup ε log N (T, d, ε).
                            t∈T         ε>0

Figure 8.1 illustrates both bounds. There is a clear gap between them, and it
turns out that metric entropy alone cannot close it – we will explore this later.
   The Dudley inequality hints that E supt∈T Xt is a multiscale quantity – to
bound it, we need to look at T across all scales ε. And that is exactly how the
proof works. We will first prove a discrete version using dyadic scales ε = 2−k
(like a Riemann sum), then move to the continuous version.
Theorem 8.1.4 (Discrete Dudley inequality). Let (Xt )t∈T be a mean-zero ran-
dom process on a metric space (T, d) with subgaussian increments as in (8.1).
Then                                    q
                                 X
                 E sup Xt ≤ CK       2−k log N (T, d, 2−k ).            (8.2)
                      t∈T
                                       k∈Z

  The proof uses the important technique of chaining. It is basicaly a multi-
scale version of the ε-net argument we have used before, like in the proofs of
Theorems 4.4.3 and 7.6.1.
  In the ε-net argument, we approximate T by an ε-net N so every point t ∈ T
224                                      Chaining




      Figure 8.1 Dudley inequality bounds E supt∈T Xt by the area under the
      curve. Sudakov inequality bounds it below by the largest area of a rectangle
      under the curve, up to constants.


is close to some π(t) ∈ N , with d(t, π(t)) ≤ ε. Then the increment condition (8.1)
gives
                                ∥Xt − Xπ(t) ∥ψ2 ≤ Kε.                                (8.3)
This leads to
                    E sup Xt ≤ E sup Xπ(t) + E sup(Xt − Xπ(t) ).
                       t∈T         t∈T              t∈T

We can handle the first term via a union bound over |N | = N (T, d, ε) points
π(t). But the second term is tricky – it is not clear how to combine (8.3) with a
union bound over all t ∈ T . To fix this, we don’t stop at one net, but we choose
smaller and smaller ε to get better approximations π1 (t), π2 (t), . . . to t with finer
nets. That is the idea behind chaining, which we will now formalize.

Proof of Theorem 8.1.4. Step 1: Chaining set-up. Without loss of generality,
we may assume that K = 1 (why?) and T is finite (see Remark 7.2.1). Define the
dyadic scale
                                  εk = 2−k ,    k∈Z                                  (8.4)
and choose εk -nets Tk of T so that
                                  |Tk | = N (T, d, εk ).                             (8.5)
Only a part of the dyadic scale will be needed. Since T is finite, there exists
a small enough number κ ∈ Z (defining the coarsest net) and a large enough
number K ∈ Z (defining the finest net), such that
                        Tκ = {t0 } for some t0 ∈ T,        TK = T.                   (8.6)
For a point t ∈ T , let πk (t) denote a closest point in Tk , so we have
                                   d(t, πk (t)) ≤ εk .                               (8.7)
                                 8.1 Dudley inequality                                 225

  Since E Xt0 = 0 by assumpiton, we have
                             E sup Xt = E sup(Xt − Xt0 ).
                               t∈T            t∈T

Let’s write Xt − Xt0 as a telescoping sum, walking from t0 to t along a chain of
points πk (t) that mark progressively finer approximations to t:
   Xt − Xt0 = (Xπκ (t) − Xt0 ) + (Xπκ+1 (t) − Xπκ (t) ) + · · · + (Xt − XπK (t) ),    (8.8)
see Figure 8.2. The first and last terms of this sum are zero by (8.6), so we have




     Figure 8.2 Chaining: a walk from a fixed point t0 to an arbitrary point t in
     T along elements πk (T ) of progressively finer nets of T


                                        K
                                        X
                         Xt − Xt0 =           (Xπk (t) − Xπk−1 (t) ).                 (8.9)
                                      k=κ+1

Since the supremum of the sum is bounded by the sum of suprema, we get
                                         K
                                         X
                 E sup(Xt − Xt0 ) ≤            E sup(Xπk (t) − Xπk−1 (t) ).          (8.10)
                   t∈T                              t∈T
                                       k=κ+1

   Step 2: Controlling the increments. In (8.10), it looks like we are taking
the supremum over all of T in each summand, but really it is over the smaller set
of pairs (πk (t), πk−1 (t)).The number of such pairs is
                                  |Tk | · |Tk−1 | ≤ |Tk |2 ,
a number that we can control through (8.5). Moreover, for a fixed t, we can bound
the increments in (8.10) like this:
   ∥Xπk (t) − Xπk−1 (t) ∥ψ2 ≤ d(πk (t), πk−1 (t)) (by (8.1) and since K = 1)
                            ≤ d(πk (t), t) + d(t, πk−1 (t)) (by triangle inequality)
                            ≤ εk + εk−1 (by (8.7))
                            ≤ 2εk−1 .
  Recall from (2.22)√that the expected maximum of N subgaussian random vari-

bound each term in (8.10):

                                                     K

                            t∈T
                                                    k=κ+1

                                               K

                      t∈T
                                              k=κ+1

Theorem 8.1.4 is proved.

                             8.1.1 Variations and Examples
Remark 8.1.5 (Dudley inequality: supremum of increments). A quick look at
the proof shows that chaining actually gives
                                        Z ∞q
                 E sup|Xt − Xt0 | ≤ CK       log N (T, d, ε) dε     (8.13)
                          t∈T                        0

for any fixed t0 ∈ T . We can combine with the same bound for Xs − Xt0 and use
triangle inequality to get:
                                        Z ∞q
                  E sup |Xt − Xs | ≤ CK        log N (T, d, ε) dε.      (8.14)
                       t,s∈T                         0
                                        8.1 Dudley inequality                                           227

Remark 8.1.6 (Dudley inequality: a high-probability bound). Dudley inequality
gives only an expectation bound, but chaining actually gives a high-probability
bound. Assuming T is finite,1 for every u ≥ 0, the bound
                            hZ ∞q                                    i
        sup |Xt − Xs | ≤ CK         log N (T, d, ε) dε + u · diam(T )   (8.15)
          t,s∈T                          0

holds with probability at least 1 − 2 exp(−u2 ) (Exercise 8.1). For Gaussian pro-
cesses, this also follows directly from Gaussian concentration (Exercise 8.2).
  Note that (8.14) and (8.15) do not need the mean zero assumption E Xt = 0,
but Dudley Theorem 8.1.3 does – otherwise it can fail. (Can you see why?)
Remark 8.1.7 (Limits of Dudley integral). Even though the Dudley integral
goes over [0, ∞], we can cap it at the diameter of T , since for ε > diam(T ) a
single ε-ball covers T and so log N (T, d, ε) = 0. Thus:
                                   Z diam(T ) q
                    E sup Xt ≤ CK               log N (T, d, ε) dε.      (8.16)
                            t∈T                  0

   If we apply Dudley inequality for the canonical Gaussian process ⟨g, t⟩, just
like we did with Sudakov inequality in Corollary 7.4.2, we get:
Theorem 8.1.8 (Dudley inequality in Rn ). The Gaussian width of any bounded
set T ⊂ Rn satisfies
                               Z ∞q
                     w(T ) ≤ C        log N (T, ε) dε.                (8.17)
                                                 0

Here N (T, ε) is the smallest number of Euclidean balls with radius ε and centers
in T that cover T .
Example 8.1.9 (Dudley is sharp for the Euclidean ball). Let’s test Dudley
inequality for the unit Euclidean ball T = B2n . Recall from (4.18) that N (B2n , ε) ≤
(3/ε)n for ε ∈ (0, 1], and of course N (B2n , ε) = 1 for ε > 1. Then
                                   Z 1q
                                                          √
                         w(B2n ) ≲      n log(3/ε) dε ≲ n.
                                             0
                                                                    √
This is optimal: as we know from (7.17), w(B2n ) ≍                      n.

integral can overestimate the Gaussian width. Here is an example:
                             n    ek                         o

with ek the standard basis vectors in Rn . Try Exercise 8.4 to show that
                                      Z ∞q

                                                     0

as n → ∞. However, the good news:

 (a) Dudley inequality is tight up to a logarithmic factor (Exercise 8.5);

   For more practice, show the log-sharpness of Dudley and Sudakov (Exer-
cise 8.5), refine the lower limit in Dudley’s integral (Exercise 8.6), and prove
subexponential (Exercise 8.7) and local (Exercise 8.8) versions of Dudley.


                    8.2 Application: empirical processes
Let’s apply Dudley inequality to empirical processes – certain natural random
processes indexed by functions. Here is a motivating example.


                       8.2.1 The Monte Carlo method
Suppose we want to compute an integral
                                  Z
                                    f dµ
                                        Ω

where f : Ω → R is a given function onR some set Ω and µ is a probability measure
                                        1
on Ω. For instance, this could just be 0 f (x) dx for a function f : [0, 1] → R (see
Figure 8.3a).
  We can do this probabilistically. Suppose X is a random point in Ω drawn
according to µ, i.e. P{X ∈ A} = µ(A) for any measurable set A ⊂ Ω. (For
instance, X ∼ Unif[0, 1] if you are integrating over [0, 1].) Then the integral
becomes the expectation:
                                Z
                                   f dµ = E f (X).
                                  Ω

  Now take i.i.d. samples X1 , X2 , . . . By the law of large numbers (Theorem 1.7.1),
                         n
                      1X
                            f (Xi ) → E f (X)    almost surely                 (8.18)
                      n i=1
as n → ∞. So, we can approximate the integral with the arithmetic mean:
                                        n
                                     1X
                           Z
                              f dµ ≈       f (Xi )                     (8.19)
                            Ω        n i=1

(see Figure 8.3b). This is the Monte Carlo method – compute the integral by
averaging function values at random sample points.
Remark 8.2.1  √ (Error rate). The expected error of the Monte Carlo estimate
(8.19) is O(1/ n). This comes from the rate of convergence in the law of large
numbers (1.23):
           n                              n
        1X                       h    1 X          i1/2    1 

                          8.2 Application: empirical processes                               229




      (a) The problem is to compute the       (b) The integral is approximated by
                                               1 Pn
      integral of f on a domain Ω.            n   1 f (Xi ) with i.i.d. random points Xi .


              Figure 8.3 Monte Carlo method for numerical integration.


Remark 8.2.2 (Monte Carlo is high-dimensional, agnostic). Monte Carlo works
well in high dimensions since the error (8.20) does not depend on dimension–
unlike grid-based integration methods. You don’t even need to know the measure
µ; just being able to sample from it is enough. Same with f – you only need its
values at a few random points.


                     8.2.2 Lipschitz law of large numbers
Can we use the same sample X1 , . . . , Xn to estimate the integral of any function
f : Ω → R? No. A badly chosen f could wiggle wildly between sample points
(like in Figure 8.4), making the Monte Carlo estimate (8.19) totally off.




     Figure 8.4 One sample X1 , . . . , Xn cannot be used to approximate the
     integral of all functions f .

  But what if we stick to functions that do not wiggle too much – like Lipschitz
ones? Then yes!
Theorem 8.2.3 (Lipschitz law of large numbers). Consider the class of functions
                          F := {f : [0, 1] → R, ∥f ∥Lip ≤ L} ,                           (8.21)
where L is any number. Let X, X1 , X2 , . . . , Xn be i.i.d. random variables taking
values in [0, 1]. Then
                                    n
                                1X                        CL
                         E sup        f (Xi ) − E f (X) ≤ √ .                            (8.22)
                           f ∈F n i=1
                                                           n
Remark 8.2.4 (One sample serves all Lipschitz functions). Before the proof,
let’s reiterate the key point: the supremum over f ∈ F is inside the expectation.
230                                       Chaining

Thanks to Markov inequality, this means that a single sample X1 , . . . , Xn will,
with high probability, work well simultaneously for√all f ∈ F. And “work well”
means approximating each integral with error O(1/ n) – same rate as the usual
law of large numbers for just one function. So, made the law of large numbers
uniform without losing anything!
   To make the proof of Theorem 8.2.3 more intuitive, it is helpful to view the
left side of (8.22) as the maximal magnitude of a random process indexed by
functions f ∈ F. Such random processes are called empirical processes:
Definition 8.2.5 (Empirical process). Let F be a class of real-valued functions
f : Ω → R on some set Ω. Let X be a random point in Ω picked according to
some probability distribution, and let X1 , X2 , . . . , Xn be independent copies of
X. The random process (Xf )f ∈F defined by
                                          n
                                       1X
                               Xf :=         f (Xi ) − E f (X)                      (8.23)
                                       n i=1
is called an empirical process indexed by F.
Proof of Theorem 8.2.3         Without loss of generality, it is enough to prove the

                        F := {f : [0, 1] → [0, 1], ∥f ∥Lip ≤ 1} .                   (8.24)

                                       Z 1q
                                     1
     E sup|Xf | = E sup|Xf − X0 | ≲ √       log N (F, ∥·∥L∞ , ε) dε. (8.25)
        f ∈F         f ∈F             n 0
(Here we used that the zero function belongs to F, and the diameter of F in the
L∞ metric is bounded by 1 by (8.24).) It is not difficult to bound the covering
numbers of F like this:
                             N (F, ∥·∥L∞ , ε) ≤ eC/ε ,
as you will check in Exercise 8.9. Substitute this bound into the integral:
                                        Z 1r
